========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It’s like everything piles up, and I don’t mean just a little clutter. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and all these lists I start and don’t finish. I’ll sit down thinking, “All right, I’m going to take care of this today,” and then I just stare at everything because I can’t figure out what needs doing first.

And then I’ll pick up one paper, put it down, start looking at another one, think maybe I should make a new list, and before I know it the whole day has gone by and nothing is really done. It’s very upsetting, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage simple things, but lately I feel buried by it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Emotionally? I’m not even sure I know how to put it neatly. It’s not just one feeling. I suppose I feel… flustered, and then ashamed that I’m flustered, if that makes sense. I look at the papers and I know they’re just papers, nothing dangerous, but inside I get this tight, awful feeling like I’ve already failed before I’ve even started.

And then I get upset with myself because I think, “Why can’t you just pick one thing and do it?” I’ve managed plenty of things in my life, you know, so it feels embarrassing to be sitting there at my age feeling defeated by a pile on the kitchen table. Sometimes I feel close to tears, not always crying exactly, but like it’s right there under the surface. Mostly I feel stuck and discouraged, and then the longer I sit there, the worse I feel because I can see the day slipping away.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. Very much so. It’s like my attention has become… I don’t know, almost loose. I can sit down with something right in front of me, like a note I’m writing or a plan for the week, and I’ll be trying to concentrate, really trying, and then if the television is on in the next room, even not very loud, my mind just sort of goes over there. I’ll start listening to the voices or the music without meaning to.

And at the senior center, if I’m filling something out or trying to keep track of what I was going to do, and two people nearby start talking, that’s it. My brain follows their conversation instead. Not because I’m trying to be nosy, it just pulls me away. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place completely, and I have to figure out what I was writing or planning all over again. It’s frustrating because it feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts if anything else is happening around me.

I can sit down with something right in front of me, like a note I’m writing or a plan for the week, and I’ll be trying to concentrate, really trying, and then if the television is on in the next room, even not very loud, my mind just sort of goes over there. I’ll start listening to the voices or the music without meaning to.\n\nAnd at the senior center, if I’m filling something out or trying to keep track of what I was going to do, and two people nearby start talking, that’s it. My brain follows their conversation instead. Not because I’m trying to be nosy, it just pulls me away. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place completely, and I have to figure out what I was writing or planning all over again. It’s frustrating because it feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts if anything else is happening around me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have trouble remembering things that you were supposed to do or appointments you had scheduled?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do, and it’s become a real problem. I used to think, “Oh, everybody forgets a thing now and then,” but this feels different because it keeps happening with things I really did mean to do.

I’ve missed returning phone calls — someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them back after lunch,” and then somehow it’s two days later and I see the little note sitting there and feel terrible. Bills have gotten left under a stack of mail because I opened them, meant to set them aside somewhere important, and then they just disappeared under everything else on the table.

And with my daughter, I’ve forgotten errands I promised her I’d do, which bothers me because I don’t want her thinking I don’t care or that I’m not listening. I really am listening in the moment. It just doesn’t seem to stay where I can get to it later.

The worst is with my husband’s appointments. More than once I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of them. I’ll have the paper, or the number written down, and I’ll tell myself I need to call, and then the day goes by and I haven’t done it. Then I feel awful, because those things matter.

Mostly I feel stuck and discouraged, and then the longer I sit there, the worse I feel because I can see the day slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very much so. It’s like my attention has become… I don’t know, almost loose. I can sit down with something right in front of me, like a note I’m writing or a plan for the week, and I’ll be trying to concentrate, really trying, and then if the television is on in the next room, even not very loud, my mind just sort of goes over there. I’ll start listening to the voices or the music without meaning to.\n\nAnd at the senior center, if I’m filling something out or trying to keep track of what I was going to do, and two people nearby start talking, that’s it. My brain follows their conversation instead. Not because I’m trying to be nosy, it just pulls me away. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place completely, and I have to figure out what I was writing or planning all over again. It’s frustrating because it feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts if anything else is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you were supposed to do or appointments you had scheduled?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, and it’s become a real problem. I used to think, “Oh, everybody forgets a thing now and then,” but this feels different because it keeps happening with things I really did mean to do.\n\nI’ve missed returning phone calls — someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them back after lunch,” and then somehow it’s two days later and I see the little note sitting there and feel terrible. Bills have gotten left under a stack of mail because I opened them, meant to set them aside somewhere important, and then they just disappeared under everything else on the table.\n\nAnd with my daughter, I’ve forgotten errands I promised her I’d do, which bothers me because I don’t want her thinking I don’t care or that I’m not listening. I really am listening in the moment. It just doesn’t seem to stay where I can get to it later.\n\nThe worst is with my husband’s appointments. More than once I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of them. I’ll have the paper, or the number written down, and I’ll tell myself I need to call, and then the day goes by and I haven’t done it. Then I feel awful, because those things matter.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel anxious?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Anxious… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t know if I would call it anxiety exactly. I do get worked up, certainly, especially when there’s too much in front of me or I feel like I’m not keeping up. But when you say “anxious,” I think of someone worrying constantly about something specific, and I don’t know that I have it that clearly.

It’s more like I get tense and unsettled, and then I start thinking I should be doing better than I am. I can sort of feel it in my body, that tightness, but I don’t always know whether that’s anxiety or just frustration, or feeling overwhelmed. Sometimes it all runs together, honestly. So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.

So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance\nmanifestation: \"When there’s a form to fill out, insurance papers to read, or a program schedule to put together, I keep finding little reasons to put it off because my head feels worn out before I even start.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. I really do, and it’s not even always because the task is physically hard or anything like that. It’s the ones where I know I have to sit and think carefully, and keep track of what each part means. Those are the ones I start avoiding.

Like if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.

Same with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.8, R41.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It’s like everything piles up, and I don’t mean just a little clutter. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and all these lists I start and don’t finish. I’ll sit down thinking, “All right, I’m going to take care of this today,” and then I just stare at everything because I can’t figure out what needs doing first.\n\nAnd then I’ll pick up one paper, put it down, start looking at another one, think maybe I should make a new list, and before I know it the whole day has gone by and nothing is really done. It’s very upsetting, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage simple things, but lately I feel buried by it.\n\nDoctor: How are you feeling emotionally when you're overwhelmed by all these tasks and papers?\n\nPatient: Emotionally? I’m not even sure I know how to put it neatly. It’s not just one feeling. I suppose I feel… flustered, and then ashamed that I’m flustered, if that makes sense. I look at the papers and I know they’re just papers, nothing dangerous, but inside I get this tight, awful feeling like I’ve already failed before I’ve even started.\n\nAnd then I get upset with myself because I think, “Why can’t you just pick one thing and do it?” I’ve managed plenty of things in my life, you know, so it feels embarrassing to be sitting there at my age feeling defeated by a pile on the kitchen table. Sometimes I feel close to tears, not always crying exactly, but like it’s right there under the surface. Mostly I feel stuck and discouraged, and then the longer I sit there, the worse I feel because I can see the day slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very much so. It’s like my attention has become… I don’t know, almost loose. I can sit down with something right in front of me, like a note I’m writing or a plan for the week, and I’ll be trying to concentrate, really trying, and then if the television is on in the next room, even not very loud, my mind just sort of goes over there. I’ll start listening to the voices or the music without meaning to.\n\nAnd at the senior center, if I’m filling something out or trying to keep track of what I was going to do, and two people nearby start talking, that’s it. My brain follows their conversation instead. Not because I’m trying to be nosy, it just pulls me away. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place completely, and I have to figure out what I was writing or planning all over again. It’s frustrating because it feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts if anything else is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you were supposed to do or appointments you had scheduled?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, and it’s become a real problem. I used to think, “Oh, everybody forgets a thing now and then,” but this feels different because it keeps happening with things I really did mean to do.\n\nI’ve missed returning phone calls — someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them back after lunch,” and then somehow it’s two days later and I see the little note sitting there and feel terrible. Bills have gotten left under a stack of mail because I opened them, meant to set them aside somewhere important, and then they just disappeared under everything else on the table.\n\nAnd with my daughter, I’ve forgotten errands I promised her I’d do, which bothers me because I don’t want her thinking I don’t care or that I’m not listening. I really am listening in the moment. It just doesn’t seem to stay where I can get to it later.\n\nThe worst is with my husband’s appointments. More than once I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of them. I’ll have the paper, or the number written down, and I’ll tell myself I need to call, and then the day goes by and I haven’t done it. Then I feel awful, because those things matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t know if I would call it anxiety exactly. I do get worked up, certainly, especially when there’s too much in front of me or I feel like I’m not keeping up. But when you say “anxious,” I think of someone worrying constantly about something specific, and I don’t know that I have it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I get tense and unsettled, and then I start thinking I should be doing better than I am. I can sort of feel it in my body, that tightness, but I don’t always know whether that’s anxiety or just frustration, or feeling overwhelmed. Sometimes it all runs together, honestly. So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I really do, and it’s not even always because the task is physically hard or anything like that. It’s the ones where I know I have to sit and think carefully, and keep track of what each part means. Those are the ones I start avoiding.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I really do, and it’s not even always because the task is physically hard or anything like that. It’s the ones where I know I have to sit and think carefully, and keep track of what each part means. Those are the ones I start avoiding.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know that I would say I’ve been “depressed” in the way people mean it clinically. I do get discouraged, especially when I feel like I’m not managing things the way I should, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being sad or depressed for a period of time.

There are days when I feel down on myself, like I’ve let people down or I’m just not doing a very good job keeping up with life. But it feels more tied to whatever has happened that day, if that makes sense, rather than a heavy sadness that stays for weeks. At least I don’t think so.

I guess I’d say I have moments of feeling low or frustrated with myself, but I haven’t really thought of it as depression. Maybe I’m not recognizing it correctly, but nothing comes to mind where I’d say, yes, I was clearly depressed for a long stretch.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It’s like everything piles up, and I don’t mean just a little clutter. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and all these lists I start and don’t finish. I’ll sit down thinking, “All right, I’m going to take care of this today,” and then I just stare at everything because I can’t figure out what needs doing first.\n\nAnd then I’ll pick up one paper, put it down, start looking at another one, think maybe I should make a new list, and before I know it the whole day has gone by and nothing is really done. It’s very upsetting, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage simple things, but lately I feel buried by it.\n\nDoctor: How are you feeling emotionally when you're overwhelmed by all these tasks and papers?\n\nPatient: Emotionally? I’m not even sure I know how to put it neatly. It’s not just one feeling. I suppose I feel… flustered, and then ashamed that I’m flustered, if that makes sense. I look at the papers and I know they’re just papers, nothing dangerous, but inside I get this tight, awful feeling like I’ve already failed before I’ve even started.\n\nAnd then I get upset with myself because I think, “Why can’t you just pick one thing and do it?” I’ve managed plenty of things in my life, you know, so it feels embarrassing to be sitting there at my age feeling defeated by a pile on the kitchen table. Sometimes I feel close to tears, not always crying exactly, but like it’s right there under the surface. Mostly I feel stuck and discouraged, and then the longer I sit there, the worse I feel because I can see the day slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very much so. It’s like my attention has become… I don’t know, almost loose. I can sit down with something right in front of me, like a note I’m writing or a plan for the week, and I’ll be trying to concentrate, really trying, and then if the television is on in the next room, even not very loud, my mind just sort of goes over there. I’ll start listening to the voices or the music without meaning to.\n\nAnd at the senior center, if I’m filling something out or trying to keep track of what I was going to do, and two people nearby start talking, that’s it. My brain follows their conversation instead. Not because I’m trying to be nosy, it just pulls me away. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place completely, and I have to figure out what I was writing or planning all over again. It’s frustrating because it feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts if anything else is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you were supposed to do or appointments you had scheduled?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, and it’s become a real problem. I used to think, “Oh, everybody forgets a thing now and then,” but this feels different because it keeps happening with things I really did mean to do.\n\nI’ve missed returning phone calls — someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them back after lunch,” and then somehow it’s two days later and I see the little note sitting there and feel terrible. Bills have gotten left under a stack of mail because I opened them, meant to set them aside somewhere important, and then they just disappeared under everything else on the table.\n\nAnd with my daughter, I’ve forgotten errands I promised her I’d do, which bothers me because I don’t want her thinking I don’t care or that I’m not listening. I really am listening in the moment. It just doesn’t seem to stay where I can get to it later.\n\nThe worst is with my husband’s appointments. More than once I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of them. I’ll have the paper, or the number written down, and I’ll tell myself I need to call, and then the day goes by and I haven’t done it. Then I feel awful, because those things matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t know if I would call it anxiety exactly. I can sort of feel it in my body, that tightness, but I don’t always know whether that’s anxiety or just frustration, or feeling overwhelmed. Sometimes it all runs together, honestly. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Inattentive Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t know if I would call it anxiety exactly. I do get worked up, certainly, especially when there’s too much in front of me or I feel like I’m not keeping up. But when you say “anxious,” I think of someone worrying constantly about something specific, and I don’t know that I have it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I get tense and unsettled, and then I start thinking I should be doing better than I am. I can sort of feel it in my body, that tightness, but I don’t always know whether that’s anxiety or just frustration, or feeling overwhelmed. Sometimes it all runs together, honestly. So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I really do, and it’s not even always because the task is physically hard or anything like that. It’s the ones where I know I have to sit and think carefully, and keep track of what each part means. Those are the ones I start avoiding.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know that I would say I’ve been “depressed” in the way people mean it clinically. I do get discouraged, especially when I feel like I’m not managing things the way I should, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being sad or depressed for a period of time.\n\nThere are days when I feel down on myself, like I’ve let people down or I’m just not doing a very good job keeping up with life. But it feels more tied to whatever has happened that day, if that makes sense, rather than a heavy sadness that stays for weeks. At least I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d say I have moments of feeling low or frustrated with myself, but I haven’t really thought of it as depression. Maybe I’m not recognizing it correctly, but nothing comes to mind where I’d say, yes, I was clearly depressed for a long stretch.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family or friends due to your current struggles?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I had to give up leading a big senior-center event, and my daughter has started double-checking bills and appointments because she no longer trusts that I can keep everything straight on my own.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I think it has affected things, more than I like to admit. I try not to make it everyone else’s problem, but it has started spilling over.

The biggest thing that comes to mind is the senior center. I was supposed to lead this larger event there—well, I had been involved before, and people sort of expected me to help organize it, keep track of the pieces, who was doing what, times, calls, that sort of thing. And I just… I couldn’t keep it all straight anymore. I kept worrying I was missing something, and honestly I was missing things. So I ended up giving it up, which was very upsetting for me. It wasn’t just the event—it made me feel like people couldn’t rely on me the way they used to.

And with my daughter, that’s been hard too. She’s kind about it, mostly, but she has started double-checking my bills and my appointments. Not in a cruel way, but I can tell she doesn’t fully trust that I’m going to keep everything organized on my own. She’ll ask, “Did you pay that?” or “Are you sure that appointment is Tuesday?” and sometimes she’ll look it over herself. I know she’s trying to help, but it makes me feel… smaller, I guess. Like I’m being watched.

So yes, I’d say it has caused some strain. Not arguments all the time, exactly, but embarrassment and tension, and this feeling that I’m losing a role I used to have.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It’s like everything piles up, and I don’t mean just a little clutter. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and all these lists I start and don’t finish. I’ll sit down thinking, “All right, I’m going to take care of this today,” and then I just stare at everything because I can’t figure out what needs doing first.\n\nAnd then I’ll pick up one paper, put it down, start looking at another one, think maybe I should make a new list, and before I know it the whole day has gone by and nothing is really done. It’s very upsetting, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage simple things, but lately I feel buried by it.\n\nDoctor: How are you feeling emotionally when you're overwhelmed by all these tasks and papers?\n\nPatient: Emotionally? I’m not even sure I know how to put it neatly. It’s not just one feeling. I suppose I feel… flustered, and then ashamed that I’m flustered, if that makes sense. I look at the papers and I know they’re just papers, nothing dangerous, but inside I get this tight, awful feeling like I’ve already failed before I’ve even started.\n\nAnd then I get upset with myself because I think, “Why can’t you just pick one thing and do it?” I’ve managed plenty of things in my life, you know, so it feels embarrassing to be sitting there at my age feeling defeated by a pile on the kitchen table. Sometimes I feel close to tears, not always crying exactly, but like it’s right there under the surface. Mostly I feel stuck and discouraged, and then the longer I sit there, the worse I feel because I can see the day slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very much so. It’s like my attention has become… I don’t know, almost loose. I can sit down with something right in front of me, like a note I’m writing or a plan for the week, and I’ll be trying to concentrate, really trying, and then if the television is on in the next room, even not very loud, my mind just sort of goes over there. I’ll start listening to the voices or the music without meaning to.\n\nAnd at the senior center, if I’m filling something out or trying to keep track of what I was going to do, and two people nearby start talking, that’s it. My brain follows their conversation instead. Not because I’m trying to be nosy, it just pulls me away. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place completely, and I have to figure out what I was writing or planning all over again. It’s frustrating because it feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts if anything else is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you were supposed to do or appointments you had scheduled?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, and it’s become a real problem. I used to think, “Oh, everybody forgets a thing now and then,” but this feels different because it keeps happening with things I really did mean to do.\n\nI’ve missed returning phone calls — someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them back after lunch,” and then somehow it’s two days later and I see the little note sitting there and feel terrible. Bills have gotten left under a stack of mail because I opened them, meant to set them aside somewhere important, and then they just disappeared under everything else on the table.\n\nAnd with my daughter, I’ve forgotten errands I promised her I’d do, which bothers me because I don’t want her thinking I don’t care or that I’m not listening. I really am listening in the moment. It just doesn’t seem to stay where I can get to it later.\n\nThe worst is with my husband’s appointments. More than once I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of them. I’ll have the paper, or the number written down, and I’ll tell myself I need to call, and then the day goes by and I haven’t done it. Then I feel awful, because those things matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t know if I would call it anxiety exactly. I do get worked up, certainly, especially when there’s too much in front of me or I feel like I’m not keeping up. So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Those are the ones I start avoiding.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. wrote that I was bright but careless, forgot books and homework, and needed reminders to finish seatwork before I could go outside.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I had to give up leading a big senior-center event, and my daughter has started double-checking bills and appointments because she no longer trusts that I can keep everything straight on my own.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 3 months\n  manifestation: It has been causing this much trouble for about a year and three months, since my husband started needing regular help with his appointments and daily arrangements.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S005\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I really do, and it’s not even always because the task is physically hard or anything like that. It’s the ones where I know I have to sit and think carefully, and keep track of what each part means. Those are the ones I start avoiding.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know that I would say I’ve been “depressed” in the way people mean it clinically. I do get discouraged, especially when I feel like I’m not managing things the way I should, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being sad or depressed for a period of time.\n\nThere are days when I feel down on myself, like I’ve let people down or I’m just not doing a very good job keeping up with life. But it feels more tied to whatever has happened that day, if that makes sense, rather than a heavy sadness that stays for weeks. At least I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d say I have moments of feeling low or frustrated with myself, but I haven’t really thought of it as depression. Maybe I’m not recognizing it correctly, but nothing comes to mind where I’d say, yes, I was clearly depressed for a long stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family or friends due to your current struggles?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has affected things, more than I like to admit. I try not to make it everyone else’s problem, but it has started spilling over.\n\nThe biggest thing that comes to mind is the senior center. I was supposed to lead this larger event there—well, I had been involved before, and people sort of expected me to help organize it, keep track of the pieces, who was doing what, times, calls, that sort of thing. And I just… I couldn’t keep it all straight anymore. I kept worrying I was missing something, and honestly I was missing things. So I ended up giving it up, which was very upsetting for me. It wasn’t just the event—it made me feel like people couldn’t rely on me the way they used to.\n\nAnd with my daughter, that’s been hard too. She’s kind about it, mostly, but she has started double-checking my bills and my appointments. Not in a cruel way, but I can tell she doesn’t fully trust that I’m going to keep everything organized on my own. She’ll ask, “Did you pay that?” or “Are you sure that appointment is Tuesday?” and sometimes she’ll look it over herself. I know she’s trying to help, but it makes me feel… smaller, I guess. Like I’m being watched.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has caused some strain. Not arguments all the time, exactly, but embarrassment and tension, and this feeling that I’m losing a role I used to have.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt like your struggles with organization and focus have significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, very much so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just little things, you know, misplacing a paper or needing to write something down, but it’s become more than that. It’s changed what I feel able to take on.

The senior center event is probably the clearest example. I had been someone people looked to for that sort of thing, helping organize a bigger event, keeping track of who was doing what, the schedule, the calls, all those moving parts. And I just couldn’t keep it straight anymore. I’d sit with the notes and still feel like I was losing pieces of it. Eventually I had to give up leading it, and that was really upsetting. It made me feel like I wasn’t dependable in a way I used to be.

And at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’ll ask if I paid something, or she’ll look at the calendar herself because she’s not sure I’ve got it right. She’s not being unkind, but it still feels awful. It makes me feel like I can’t be fully trusted with my own life, if that makes sense.

So yes, it has affected my daily life, and my well-being too. I feel embarrassed and less confident, and I don’t feel like myself in the same way.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.8, R41.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It’s like everything piles up, and I don’t mean just a little clutter. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and all these lists I start and don’t finish. I’ll sit down thinking, “All right, I’m going to take care of this today,” and then I just stare at everything because I can’t figure out what needs doing first.\n\nAnd then I’ll pick up one paper, put it down, start looking at another one, think maybe I should make a new list, and before I know it the whole day has gone by and nothing is really done. It’s very upsetting, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage simple things, but lately I feel buried by it.\n\nDoctor: How are you feeling emotionally when you're overwhelmed by all these tasks and papers?\n\nPatient: Emotionally? I’m not even sure I know how to put it neatly. It’s not just one feeling. I suppose I feel… flustered, and then ashamed that I’m flustered, if that makes sense. I look at the papers and I know they’re just papers, nothing dangerous, but inside I get this tight, awful feeling like I’ve already failed before I’ve even started.\n\nAnd then I get upset with myself because I think, “Why can’t you just pick one thing and do it?” I’ve managed plenty of things in my life, you know, so it feels embarrassing to be sitting there at my age feeling defeated by a pile on the kitchen table. Sometimes I feel close to tears, not always crying exactly, but like it’s right there under the surface. Mostly I feel stuck and discouraged, and then the longer I sit there, the worse I feel because I can see the day slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very much so. It’s like my attention has become… I don’t know, almost loose. I can sit down with something right in front of me, like a note I’m writing or a plan for the week, and I’ll be trying to concentrate, really trying, and then if the television is on in the next room, even not very loud, my mind just sort of goes over there. I’ll start listening to the voices or the music without meaning to.\n\nAnd at the senior center, if I’m filling something out or trying to keep track of what I was going to do, and two people nearby start talking, that’s it. My brain follows their conversation instead. Not because I’m trying to be nosy, it just pulls me away. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place completely, and I have to figure out what I was writing or planning all over again. It’s frustrating because it feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts if anything else is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you were supposed to do or appointments you had scheduled?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, and it’s become a real problem. I used to think, “Oh, everybody forgets a thing now and then,” but this feels different because it keeps happening with things I really did mean to do.\n\nI’ve missed returning phone calls — someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them back after lunch,” and then somehow it’s two days later and I see the little note sitting there and feel terrible. Bills have gotten left under a stack of mail because I opened them, meant to set them aside somewhere important, and then they just disappeared under everything else on the table.\n\nAnd with my daughter, I’ve forgotten errands I promised her I’d do, which bothers me because I don’t want her thinking I don’t care or that I’m not listening. I really am listening in the moment. It just doesn’t seem to stay where I can get to it later.\n\nThe worst is with my husband’s appointments. More than once I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of them. I’ll have the paper, or the number written down, and I’ll tell myself I need to call, and then the day goes by and I haven’t done it. Then I feel awful, because those things matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t know if I would call it anxiety exactly. I do get worked up, certainly, especially when there’s too much in front of me or I feel like I’m not keeping up. But when you say “anxious,” I think of someone worrying constantly about something specific, and I don’t know that I have it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I get tense and unsettled, and then I start thinking I should be doing better than I am. I can sort of feel it in my body, that tightness, but I don’t always know whether that’s anxiety or just frustration, or feeling overwhelmed. Sometimes it all runs together, honestly. So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I really do, and it’s not even always because the task is physically hard or anything like that. It’s the ones where I know I have to sit and think carefully, and keep track of what each part means. Those are the ones I start avoiding.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know that I would say I’ve been “depressed” in the way people mean it clinically. I do get discouraged, especially when I feel like I’m not managing things the way I should, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being sad or depressed for a period of time.\n\nThere are days when I feel down on myself, like I’ve let people down or I’m just not doing a very good job keeping up with life. But it feels more tied to whatever has happened that day, if that makes sense, rather than a heavy sadness that stays for weeks. At least I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d say I have moments of feeling low or frustrated with myself, but I haven’t really thought of it as depression. Maybe I’m not recognizing it correctly, but nothing comes to mind where I’d say, yes, I was clearly depressed for a long stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family or friends due to your current struggles?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has affected things, more than I like to admit. I try not to make it everyone else’s problem, but it has started spilling over.\n\nThe biggest thing that comes to mind is the senior center. I was supposed to lead this larger event there—well, I had been involved before, and people sort of expected me to help organize it, keep track of the pieces, who was doing what, times, calls, that sort of thing. And I just… I couldn’t keep it all straight anymore. I kept worrying I was missing something, and honestly I was missing things. So I ended up giving it up, which was very upsetting for me. It wasn’t just the event—it made me feel like people couldn’t rely on me the way they used to.\n\nAnd with my daughter, that’s been hard too. She’s kind about it, mostly, but she has started double-checking my bills and my appointments. Not in a cruel way, but I can tell she doesn’t fully trust that I’m going to keep everything organized on my own. She’ll ask, “Did you pay that?” or “Are you sure that appointment is Tuesday?” and sometimes she’ll look it over herself. I know she’s trying to help, but it makes me feel… smaller, I guess. Like I’m being watched.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has caused some strain. Not arguments all the time, exactly, but embarrassment and tension, and this feeling that I’m losing a role I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your struggles with organization and focus have significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just little things, you know, misplacing a paper or needing to write something down, but it’s become more than that. It’s changed what I feel able to take on.\n\nThe senior center event is probably the clearest example. I had been someone people looked to for that sort of thing, helping organize a bigger event, keeping track of who was doing what, the schedule, the calls, all those moving parts. And I just couldn’t keep it straight anymore. I’d sit with the notes and still feel like I was losing pieces of it. Eventually I had to give up leading it, and that was really upsetting. It made me feel like I wasn’t dependable in a way I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’ll ask if I paid something, or she’ll look at the calendar herself because she’s not sure I’ve got it right. She’s not being unkind, but it still feels awful. It makes me feel like I can’t be fully trusted with my own life, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it has affected my daily life, and my well-being too. I feel embarrassed and less confident, and I don’t feel like myself in the same way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I can sort of feel it in my body, that tightness, but I don’t always know whether that’s anxiety or just frustration, or feeling overwhelmed. Sometimes it all runs together, honestly. So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I really do, and it’s not even always because the task is physically hard or anything like that. It’s the ones where I know I have to sit and think carefully, and keep track of what each part means. Those are the ones I start avoiding.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know that I would say I’ve been “depressed” in the way people mean it clinically. I do get discouraged, especially when I feel like I’m not managing things the way I should, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being sad or depressed for a period of time.\n\nThere are days when I feel down on myself, like I’ve let people down or I’m just not doing a very good job keeping up with life. But it feels more tied to whatever has happened that day, if that makes sense, rather than a heavy sadness that stays for weeks. At least I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d say I have moments of feeling low or frustrated with myself, but I haven’t really thought of it as depression. Maybe I’m not recognizing it correctly, but nothing comes to mind where I’d say, yes, I was clearly depressed for a long stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family or friends due to your current struggles?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has affected things, more than I like to admit. I try not to make it everyone else’s problem, but it has started spilling over.\n\nThe biggest thing that comes to mind is the senior center. I was supposed to lead this larger event there—well, I had been involved before, and people sort of expected me to help organize it, keep track of the pieces, who was doing what, times, calls, that sort of thing. And I just… I couldn’t keep it all straight anymore. I kept worrying I was missing something, and honestly I was missing things. So I ended up giving it up, which was very upsetting for me. It wasn’t just the event—it made me feel like people couldn’t rely on me the way they used to.\n\nAnd with my daughter, that’s been hard too. She’s kind about it, mostly, but she has started double-checking my bills and my appointments. Not in a cruel way, but I can tell she doesn’t fully trust that I’m going to keep everything organized on my own. She’ll ask, “Did you pay that?” or “Are you sure that appointment is Tuesday?” and sometimes she’ll look it over herself. I know she’s trying to help, but it makes me feel… smaller, I guess. Like I’m being watched.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has caused some strain. Not arguments all the time, exactly, but embarrassment and tension, and this feeling that I’m losing a role I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your struggles with organization and focus have significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just little things, you know, misplacing a paper or needing to write something down, but it’s become more than that. It’s changed what I feel able to take on.\n\nThe senior center event is probably the clearest example. I had been someone people looked to for that sort of thing, helping organize a bigger event, keeping track of who was doing what, the schedule, the calls, all those moving parts. And I just couldn’t keep it straight anymore. I’d sit with the notes and still feel like I was losing pieces of it. Eventually I had to give up leading it, and that was really upsetting. It made me feel like I wasn’t dependable in a way I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’ll ask if I paid something, or she’ll look at the calendar herself because she’s not sure I’ve got it right. She’s not being unkind, but it still feels awful. It makes me feel like I can’t be fully trusted with my own life, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it has affected my daily life, and my well-being too. I feel embarrassed and less confident, and I don’t feel like myself in the same way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean… maybe sometimes, but not in a constant way where I’m walking around feeling guilty all day. It’s more that I get disappointed in myself, or embarrassed, when I realize I didn’t handle something the way I think I should have.

Like I’ll think, “You should be able to do this,” or “Why is this so hard for you now?” And that can feel pretty bad. But guilt, to me, sounds like I’ve done something wrong to someone on purpose, and I don’t usually feel it that way. It’s more of a nagging feeling that I ought to be doing better, that I should have kept track of things or followed through more smoothly.

So… yes and no. I don’t know if I’d use the word guilty, but I do beat myself up a bit. Maybe more than I should.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.8, R41.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It’s like everything piles up, and I don’t mean just a little clutter. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and all these lists I start and don’t finish. I’ll sit down thinking, “All right, I’m going to take care of this today,” and then I just stare at everything because I can’t figure out what needs doing first.\n\nAnd then I’ll pick up one paper, put it down, start looking at another one, think maybe I should make a new list, and before I know it the whole day has gone by and nothing is really done. It’s very upsetting, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage simple things, but lately I feel buried by it.\n\nDoctor: How are you feeling emotionally when you're overwhelmed by all these tasks and papers?\n\nPatient: Emotionally? I’m not even sure I know how to put it neatly. It’s not just one feeling. I suppose I feel… flustered, and then ashamed that I’m flustered, if that makes sense. I look at the papers and I know they’re just papers, nothing dangerous, but inside I get this tight, awful feeling like I’ve already failed before I’ve even started.\n\nAnd then I get upset with myself because I think, “Why can’t you just pick one thing and do it?” I’ve managed plenty of things in my life, you know, so it feels embarrassing to be sitting there at my age feeling defeated by a pile on the kitchen table. Sometimes I feel close to tears, not always crying exactly, but like it’s right there under the surface. Mostly I feel stuck and discouraged, and then the longer I sit there, the worse I feel because I can see the day slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very much so. It’s like my attention has become… I don’t know, almost loose. I can sit down with something right in front of me, like a note I’m writing or a plan for the week, and I’ll be trying to concentrate, really trying, and then if the television is on in the next room, even not very loud, my mind just sort of goes over there. I’ll start listening to the voices or the music without meaning to.\n\nAnd at the senior center, if I’m filling something out or trying to keep track of what I was going to do, and two people nearby start talking, that’s it. My brain follows their conversation instead. Not because I’m trying to be nosy, it just pulls me away. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place completely, and I have to figure out what I was writing or planning all over again. It’s frustrating because it feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts if anything else is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you were supposed to do or appointments you had scheduled?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, and it’s become a real problem. I used to think, “Oh, everybody forgets a thing now and then,” but this feels different because it keeps happening with things I really did mean to do.\n\nI’ve missed returning phone calls — someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them back after lunch,” and then somehow it’s two days later and I see the little note sitting there and feel terrible. Bills have gotten left under a stack of mail because I opened them, meant to set them aside somewhere important, and then they just disappeared under everything else on the table.\n\nAnd with my daughter, I’ve forgotten errands I promised her I’d do, which bothers me because I don’t want her thinking I don’t care or that I’m not listening. I really am listening in the moment. It just doesn’t seem to stay where I can get to it later.\n\nThe worst is with my husband’s appointments. More than once I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of them. I’ll have the paper, or the number written down, and I’ll tell myself I need to call, and then the day goes by and I haven’t done it. Then I feel awful, because those things matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t know if I would call it anxiety exactly. I do get worked up, certainly, especially when there’s too much in front of me or I feel like I’m not keeping up. But when you say “anxious,” I think of someone worrying constantly about something specific, and I don’t know that I have it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I get tense and unsettled, and then I start thinking I should be doing better than I am. I can sort of feel it in my body, that tightness, but I don’t always know whether that’s anxiety or just frustration, or feeling overwhelmed. Sometimes it all runs together, honestly. So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I really do, and it’s not even always because the task is physically hard or anything like that. It’s the ones where I know I have to sit and think carefully, and keep track of what each part means. Those are the ones I start avoiding.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know that I would say I’ve been “depressed” in the way people mean it clinically. I do get discouraged, especially when I feel like I’m not managing things the way I should, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being sad or depressed for a period of time.\n\nThere are days when I feel down on myself, like I’ve let people down or I’m just not doing a very good job keeping up with life. But it feels more tied to whatever has happened that day, if that makes sense, rather than a heavy sadness that stays for weeks. At least I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d say I have moments of feeling low or frustrated with myself, but I haven’t really thought of it as depression. Maybe I’m not recognizing it correctly, but nothing comes to mind where I’d say, yes, I was clearly depressed for a long stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family or friends due to your current struggles?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has affected things, more than I like to admit. I try not to make it everyone else’s problem, but it has started spilling over.\n\nThe biggest thing that comes to mind is the senior center. I was supposed to lead this larger event there—well, I had been involved before, and people sort of expected me to help organize it, keep track of the pieces, who was doing what, times, calls, that sort of thing. And I just… I couldn’t keep it all straight anymore. I kept worrying I was missing something, and honestly I was missing things. So I ended up giving it up, which was very upsetting for me. It wasn’t just the event—it made me feel like people couldn’t rely on me the way they used to.\n\nAnd with my daughter, that’s been hard too. She’s kind about it, mostly, but she has started double-checking my bills and my appointments. Not in a cruel way, but I can tell she doesn’t fully trust that I’m going to keep everything organized on my own. She’ll ask, “Did you pay that?” or “Are you sure that appointment is Tuesday?” and sometimes she’ll look it over herself. I know she’s trying to help, but it makes me feel… smaller, I guess. Like I’m being watched.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has caused some strain. Not arguments all the time, exactly, but embarrassment and tension, and this feeling that I’m losing a role I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your struggles with organization and focus have significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just little things, you know, misplacing a paper or needing to write something down, but it’s become more than that. It’s changed what I feel able to take on.\n\nThe senior center event is probably the clearest example. I had been someone people looked to for that sort of thing, helping organize a bigger event, keeping track of who was doing what, the schedule, the calls, all those moving parts. And I just couldn’t keep it straight anymore. I’d sit with the notes and still feel like I was losing pieces of it. Eventually I had to give up leading it, and that was really upsetting. It made me feel like I wasn’t dependable in a way I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’ll ask if I paid something, or she’ll look at the calendar herself because she’s not sure I’ve got it right. She’s not being unkind, but it still feels awful. It makes me feel like I can’t be fully trusted with my own life, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it has affected my daily life, and my well-being too. I feel embarrassed and less confident, and I don’t feel like myself in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you think you should be doing better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean… maybe sometimes, but not in a constant way where I’m walking around feeling guilty all day. It’s more that I get disappointed in myself, or embarrassed, when I realize I didn’t handle something the way I think I should have.\n\nLike I’ll think, “You should be able to do this,” or “Why is this so hard for you now?” And that can feel pretty bad. But guilt, to me, sounds like I’ve done something wrong to someone on purpose, and I don’t usually feel it that way. It’s more of a nagging feeling that I ought to be doing better, that I should have kept track of things or followed through more smoothly.\n\nSo… yes and no. I don’t know if I’d use the word guilty, but I do beat myself up a bit. Maybe more than I should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s very upsetting, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage simple things, but lately I feel buried by it.\n\nDoctor: How are you feeling emotionally when you're overwhelmed by all these tasks and papers?\n\nPatient: Emotionally?
