    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F48.8, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It’s like everything piles up, and I don’t mean just a little clutter. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and all these lists I start and don’t finish. I’ll sit down thinking, “All right, I’m going to take care of this today,” and then I just stare at everything because I can’t figure out what needs doing first.\n\nAnd then I’ll pick up one paper, put it down, start looking at another one, think maybe I should make a new list, and before I know it the whole day has gone by and nothing is really done. It’s very upsetting, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage simple things, but lately I feel buried by it.\n\nDoctor: How are you feeling emotionally when you're overwhelmed by all these tasks and papers?\n\nPatient: Emotionally? I’m not even sure I know how to put it neatly. It’s not just one feeling. I suppose I feel… flustered, and then ashamed that I’m flustered, if that makes sense. I look at the papers and I know they’re just papers, nothing dangerous, but inside I get this tight, awful feeling like I’ve already failed before I’ve even started.\n\nAnd then I get upset with myself because I think, “Why can’t you just pick one thing and do it?” I’ve managed plenty of things in my life, you know, so it feels embarrassing to be sitting there at my age feeling defeated by a pile on the kitchen table. Sometimes I feel close to tears, not always crying exactly, but like it’s right there under the surface. Mostly I feel stuck and discouraged, and then the longer I sit there, the worse I feel because I can see the day slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very much so. It’s like my attention has become… I don’t know, almost loose. I can sit down with something right in front of me, like a note I’m writing or a plan for the week, and I’ll be trying to concentrate, really trying, and then if the television is on in the next room, even not very loud, my mind just sort of goes over there. I’ll start listening to the voices or the music without meaning to.\n\nAnd at the senior center, if I’m filling something out or trying to keep track of what I was going to do, and two people nearby start talking, that’s it. My brain follows their conversation instead. Not because I’m trying to be nosy, it just pulls me away. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place completely, and I have to figure out what I was writing or planning all over again. It’s frustrating because it feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts if anything else is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you were supposed to do or appointments you had scheduled?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, and it’s become a real problem. I used to think, “Oh, everybody forgets a thing now and then,” but this feels different because it keeps happening with things I really did mean to do.\n\nI’ve missed returning phone calls — someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them back after lunch,” and then somehow it’s two days later and I see the little note sitting there and feel terrible. Bills have gotten left under a stack of mail because I opened them, meant to set them aside somewhere important, and then they just disappeared under everything else on the table.\n\nAnd with my daughter, I’ve forgotten errands I promised her I’d do, which bothers me because I don’t want her thinking I don’t care or that I’m not listening. I really am listening in the moment. It just doesn’t seem to stay where I can get to it later.\n\nThe worst is with my husband’s appointments. More than once I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of them. I’ll have the paper, or the number written down, and I’ll tell myself I need to call, and then the day goes by and I haven’t done it. Then I feel awful, because those things matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t know if I would call it anxiety exactly. I do get worked up, certainly, especially when there’s too much in front of me or I feel like I’m not keeping up. But when you say “anxious,” I think of someone worrying constantly about something specific, and I don’t know that I have it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I get tense and unsettled, and then I start thinking I should be doing better than I am. I can sort of feel it in my body, that tightness, but I don’t always know whether that’s anxiety or just frustration, or feeling overwhelmed. Sometimes it all runs together, honestly. So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I really do, and it’s not even always because the task is physically hard or anything like that. It’s the ones where I know I have to sit and think carefully, and keep track of what each part means. Those are the ones I start avoiding.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know that I would say I’ve been “depressed” in the way people mean it clinically. I do get discouraged, especially when I feel like I’m not managing things the way I should, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being sad or depressed for a period of time.\n\nThere are days when I feel down on myself, like I’ve let people down or I’m just not doing a very good job keeping up with life. But it feels more tied to whatever has happened that day, if that makes sense, rather than a heavy sadness that stays for weeks. At least I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d say I have moments of feeling low or frustrated with myself, but I haven’t really thought of it as depression. Maybe I’m not recognizing it correctly, but nothing comes to mind where I’d say, yes, I was clearly depressed for a long stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family or friends due to your current struggles?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has affected things, more than I like to admit. I try not to make it everyone else’s problem, but it has started spilling over.\n\nThe biggest thing that comes to mind is the senior center. I was supposed to lead this larger event there—well, I had been involved before, and people sort of expected me to help organize it, keep track of the pieces, who was doing what, times, calls, that sort of thing. And I just… I couldn’t keep it all straight anymore. I kept worrying I was missing something, and honestly I was missing things. So I ended up giving it up, which was very upsetting for me. It wasn’t just the event—it made me feel like people couldn’t rely on me the way they used to.\n\nAnd with my daughter, that’s been hard too. She’s kind about it, mostly, but she has started double-checking my bills and my appointments. Not in a cruel way, but I can tell she doesn’t fully trust that I’m going to keep everything organized on my own. She’ll ask, “Did you pay that?” or “Are you sure that appointment is Tuesday?” and sometimes she’ll look it over herself. I know she’s trying to help, but it makes me feel… smaller, I guess. Like I’m being watched.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has caused some strain. Not arguments all the time, exactly, but embarrassment and tension, and this feeling that I’m losing a role I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your struggles with organization and focus have significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just little things, you know, misplacing a paper or needing to write something down, but it’s become more than that. It’s changed what I feel able to take on.\n\nThe senior center event is probably the clearest example. I had been someone people looked to for that sort of thing, helping organize a bigger event, keeping track of who was doing what, the schedule, the calls, all those moving parts. And I just couldn’t keep it straight anymore. I’d sit with the notes and still feel like I was losing pieces of it. Eventually I had to give up leading it, and that was really upsetting. It made me feel like I wasn’t dependable in a way I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’ll ask if I paid something, or she’ll look at the calendar herself because she’s not sure I’ve got it right. She’s not being unkind, but it still feels awful. It makes me feel like I can’t be fully trusted with my own life, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it has affected my daily life, and my well-being too. I feel embarrassed and less confident, and I don’t feel like myself in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you think you should be doing better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean… maybe sometimes, but not in a constant way where I’m walking around feeling guilty all day. It’s more that I get disappointed in myself, or embarrassed, when I realize I didn’t handle something the way I think I should have.\n\nLike I’ll think, “You should be able to do this,” or “Why is this so hard for you now?” And that can feel pretty bad. But guilt, to me, sounds like I’ve done something wrong to someone on purpose, and I don’t usually feel it that way. It’s more of a nagging feeling that I ought to be doing better, that I should have kept track of things or followed through more smoothly.\n\nSo… yes and no. I don’t know if I’d use the word guilty, but I do beat myself up a bit. Maybe more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I don’t always notice it right away. It’s more that I catch myself doing things with my hands or feet when I’m supposed to be sitting still.\n\nLike if I’m in a meeting, or somewhere I’m trying to sit politely and pay attention, I’ll realize I’ve been rubbing my hands together, or tapping my foot, or clicking a pen if I have one. Sometimes I shift around in the chair so much that I can tell people notice—I’ll see someone glance over, and then I feel embarrassed and try to stop. But then a few minutes later I’m doing something else, like moving my fingers or adjusting how I’m sitting again.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be disruptive. It’s almost like my body is busy even when I’m trying to look calm. And at my age, I feel a little silly saying that, but yes, I would say I do get restless or fidgety.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do that very often, no. At least, not in the way I think you mean, like jumping in over people all the time or answering before they’ve even finished. I’m usually more worried about saying the wrong thing, honestly, so I tend to wait.\n\nThat said, sometimes in a conversation I may start to speak because I think the person is finished, and then I realize they weren’t, and I feel bad and stop myself. Or I’ll have a thought come into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I might start to say it a little too quickly. But I wouldn’t say people complain that I interrupt them a lot, and I don’t think of myself as blurting things out.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the bigger problems I notice.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with tasks that require sustained mental effort over a long period?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it’s not like the task is physically hard, you know? It’s paperwork, or reading something carefully, or organizing information, and I’ll look at it and feel tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I can almost feel my mind backing away from it. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just make some tea first,” or “I need to clear off the table,” or “I’ll do it after lunch when I can concentrate better.” And then after lunch there’s another reason, and then it becomes tomorrow. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just avoiding responsibility, but inside it feels like my head is already worn out just thinking about all the little parts I have to keep track of.\n\nEven something like putting together a schedule for a program, where I have to look at times and names and make sure everything lines up, it can feel like too much mental lifting. I know it needs to be done, and I know I’m capable in some basic sense, but sustaining that kind of effort for a long stretch is very difficult for me. I keep drifting away from it or finding small reasons to stop before I really get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I don’t think of myself as someone who has a big problem waiting my turn, at least not in an obvious way.\n\nIn conversations, I usually try to be polite and let people finish. Sometimes I may be sitting there with something I want to say and I’m afraid I’ll forget it, so internally I might feel a little impatient, like I’m holding onto the thought. But I don’t think I’m usually pushing ahead of people or demanding my turn right away.\n\nWith activities, like waiting in line or waiting for someone else to finish something, I can get annoyed or restless like anyone, but I don’t know that it’s more than normal. It’s not something people have pointed out to me. So I’d say… maybe I feel impatient inside sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed a major problem with waiting my turn.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or blame yourself for things that happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “worthless,” exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do blame myself sometimes, though, especially if something doesn’t get done or I’ve made things harder for someone else. I can go over it in my head and think, “Why didn’t I just handle that better?” or “Why is this so difficult for me when other people seem to manage it?”\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as feeling worthless. I think it’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I feel guilty or ashamed. I can be hard on myself, probably more than I need to be. But if you mean do I sit around believing I have no value as a person, I don’t think I’d describe it that way. It’s more… I disappoint myself, and then I carry that around longer than I should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I know it needs to be done, and I know I’m capable in some basic sense, but sustaining that kind of effort for a long stretch is very difficult for me. I keep drifting away from it or finding small reasons to stop before I really get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I don’t think of myself as someone who has a big problem waiting my turn, at least not in an obvious way.\n\nIn conversations, I usually try to be polite and let people finish. Sometimes I may be sitting there with something I want to say and I’m afraid I’ll forget it, so internally I might feel a little impatient, like I’m holding onto the thought. But I don’t think I’m usually pushing ahead of people or demanding my turn right away.\n\nWith activities, like waiting in line or waiting for someone else to finish something, I can get annoyed or restless like anyone, but I don’t know that it’s more than normal. It’s not something people have pointed out to me. So I’d say… maybe I feel impatient inside sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed a major problem with waiting my turn.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or blame yourself for things that happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “worthless,” exactly. I can go over it in my head and think, “Why didn’t I just handle that better?” or “Why is this so difficult for me when other people seem to manage it?”\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as feeling worthless. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it’s definitely not recent. I mean, I’m 70 now, so when I look back it’s a little strange, because for years I thought it was just my personality or that I was messy or not trying hard enough. But it goes way back.

Even when I was a little girl, my teachers would write things like I was bright but careless. That phrase stuck with me, actually — “bright but careless” — because it followed me around in one form or another. I’d forget my books, forget homework, lose track of what I was supposed to bring in, and I remember needing reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. It wasn’t that I didn’t understand the work. A lot of the time I did understand it. I just didn’t have myself organized enough to get it done and turned in the way other children seemed to.

So, no, this isn’t something that suddenly started in old age or after retirement. It’s been a lifelong pattern, really. The circumstances have changed over the years, but that basic difficulty with keeping things organized and managing what needs to be done has been there since childhood.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It’s like everything piles up, and I don’t mean just a little clutter. It’s very upsetting, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage simple things, but lately I feel buried by it.\n\nDoctor: How are you feeling emotionally when you're overwhelmed by all these tasks and papers?\n\nPatient: Emotionally? I’m not even sure I know how to put it neatly. It’s not just one feeling. I suppose I feel… flustered, and then ashamed that I’m flustered, if that makes sense. I look at the papers and I know they’re just papers, nothing dangerous, but inside I get this tight, awful feeling like I’ve already failed before I’ve even started.\n\nAnd then I get upset with myself because I think, “Why can’t you just pick one thing and do it?” I’ve managed plenty of things in my life, you know, so it feels embarrassing to be sitting there at my age feeling defeated by a pile on the kitchen table. Mostly I feel stuck and discouraged, and then the longer I sit there, the worse I feel because I can see the day slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very much so. It’s like my attention has become… I don’t know, almost loose. I can sit down with something right in front of me, like a note I’m writing or a plan for the week, and I’ll be trying to concentrate, really trying, and then if the television is on in the next room, even not very loud, my mind just sort of goes over there. I’ll start listening to the voices or the music without meaning to.\n\nAnd at the senior center, if I’m filling something out or trying to keep track of what I was going to do, and two people nearby start talking, that’s it. My brain follows their conversation instead. Not because I’m trying to be nosy, it just pulls me away. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place completely, and I have to figure out what I was writing or planning all over again. It’s frustrating because it feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts if anything else is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you were supposed to do or appointments you had scheduled?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, and it’s become a real problem. I used to think, “Oh, everybody forgets a thing now and then,” but this feels different because it keeps happening with things I really did mean to do.\n\nI’ve missed returning phone calls — someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them back after lunch,” and then somehow it’s two days later and I see the little note sitting there and feel terrible. Bills have gotten left under a stack of mail because I opened them, meant to set them aside somewhere important, and then they just disappeared under everything else on the table.\n\nAnd with my daughter, I’ve forgotten errands I promised her I’d do, which bothers me because I don’t want her thinking I don’t care or that I’m not listening. I really am listening in the moment. It just doesn’t seem to stay where I can get to it later.\n\nThe worst is with my husband’s appointments. More than once I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of them. I’ll have the paper, or the number written down, and I’ll tell myself I need to call, and then the day goes by and I haven’t done it. Then I feel awful, because those things matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t know if I would call it anxiety exactly. I do get worked up, certainly, especially when there’s too much in front of me or I feel like I’m not keeping up. But when you say “anxious,” I think of someone worrying constantly about something specific, and I don’t know that I have it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I get tense and unsettled, and then I start thinking I should be doing better than I am. I can sort of feel it in my body, that tightness, but I don’t always know whether that’s anxiety or just frustration, or feeling overwhelmed. Sometimes it all runs together, honestly. So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I really do, and it’s not even always because the task is physically hard or anything like that. It’s the ones where I know I have to sit and think carefully, and keep track of what each part means. Those are the ones I start avoiding.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know that I would say I’ve been “depressed” in the way people mean it clinically. I do get discouraged, especially when I feel like I’m not managing things the way I should, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being sad or depressed for a period of time.\n\nThere are days when I feel down on myself, like I’ve let people down or I’m just not doing a very good job keeping up with life. But it feels more tied to whatever has happened that day, if that makes sense, rather than a heavy sadness that stays for weeks. At least I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d say I have moments of feeling low or frustrated with myself, but I haven’t really thought of it as depression. Maybe I’m not recognizing it correctly, but nothing comes to mind where I’d say, yes, I was clearly depressed for a long stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family or friends due to your current struggles?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has affected things, more than I like to admit. I try not to make it everyone else’s problem, but it has started spilling over.\n\nThe biggest thing that comes to mind is the senior center. I was supposed to lead this larger event there—well, I had been involved before, and people sort of expected me to help organize it, keep track of the pieces, who was doing what, times, calls, that sort of thing. And I just… I couldn’t keep it all straight anymore. I kept worrying I was missing something, and honestly I was missing things. So I ended up giving it up, which was very upsetting for me. It wasn’t just the event—it made me feel like people couldn’t rely on me the way they used to.\n\nAnd with my daughter, that’s been hard too. She’s kind about it, mostly, but she has started double-checking my bills and my appointments. Not in a cruel way, but I can tell she doesn’t fully trust that I’m going to keep everything organized on my own. She’ll ask, “Did you pay that?” or “Are you sure that appointment is Tuesday?” and sometimes she’ll look it over herself. I know she’s trying to help, but it makes me feel… smaller, I guess. Like I’m being watched.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has caused some strain. Not arguments all the time, exactly, but embarrassment and tension, and this feeling that I’m losing a role I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your struggles with organization and focus have significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just little things, you know, misplacing a paper or needing to write something down, but it’s become more than that. It’s changed what I feel able to take on.\n\nThe senior center event is probably the clearest example. I had been someone people looked to for that sort of thing, helping organize a bigger event, keeping track of who was doing what, the schedule, the calls, all those moving parts. And I just couldn’t keep it straight anymore. I’d sit with the notes and still feel like I was losing pieces of it. Eventually I had to give up leading it, and that was really upsetting. It made me feel like I wasn’t dependable in a way I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’ll ask if I paid something, or she’ll look at the calendar herself because she’s not sure I’ve got it right. She’s not being unkind, but it still feels awful. It makes me feel like I can’t be fully trusted with my own life, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it has affected my daily life, and my well-being too. I feel embarrassed and less confident, and I don’t feel like myself in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you think you should be doing better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean… maybe sometimes, but not in a constant way where I’m walking around feeling guilty all day. It’s more that I get disappointed in myself, or embarrassed, when I realize I didn’t handle something the way I think I should have.\n\nLike I’ll think, “You should be able to do this,” or “Why is this so hard for you now?” And that can feel pretty bad. But guilt, to me, sounds like I’ve done something wrong to someone on purpose, and I don’t usually feel it that way. It’s more of a nagging feeling that I ought to be doing better, that I should have kept track of things or followed through more smoothly.\n\nSo… yes and no. I don’t know if I’d use the word guilty, but I do beat myself up a bit. Maybe more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I don’t always notice it right away. It’s more that I catch myself doing things with my hands or feet when I’m supposed to be sitting still.\n\nLike if I’m in a meeting, or somewhere I’m trying to sit politely and pay attention, I’ll realize I’ve been rubbing my hands together, or tapping my foot, or clicking a pen if I have one. Sometimes I shift around in the chair so much that I can tell people notice—I’ll see someone glance over, and then I feel embarrassed and try to stop. But then a few minutes later I’m doing something else, like moving my fingers or adjusting how I’m sitting again.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be disruptive. It’s almost like my body is busy even when I’m trying to look calm. And at my age, I feel a little silly saying that, but yes, I would say I do get restless or fidgety.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do that very often, no. At least, not in the way I think you mean, like jumping in over people all the time or answering before they’ve even finished. I’m usually more worried about saying the wrong thing, honestly, so I tend to wait.\n\nThat said, sometimes in a conversation I may start to speak because I think the person is finished, and then I realize they weren’t, and I feel bad and stop myself. Or I’ll have a thought come into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I might start to say it a little too quickly. But I wouldn’t say people complain that I interrupt them a lot, and I don’t think of myself as blurting things out.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the bigger problems I notice.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with tasks that require sustained mental effort over a long period?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it’s not like the task is physically hard, you know? It’s paperwork, or reading something carefully, or organizing information, and I’ll look at it and feel tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I can almost feel my mind backing away from it. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just make some tea first,” or “I need to clear off the table,” or “I’ll do it after lunch when I can concentrate better.” And then after lunch there’s another reason, and then it becomes tomorrow. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just avoiding responsibility, but inside it feels like my head is already worn out just thinking about all the little parts I have to keep track of.\n\nEven something like putting together a schedule for a program, where I have to look at times and names and make sure everything lines up, it can feel like too much mental lifting. I know it needs to be done, and I know I’m capable in some basic sense, but sustaining that kind of effort for a long stretch is very difficult for me. I keep drifting away from it or finding small reasons to stop before I really get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I don’t think of myself as someone who has a big problem waiting my turn, at least not in an obvious way.\n\nIn conversations, I usually try to be polite and let people finish. Sometimes I may be sitting there with something I want to say and I’m afraid I’ll forget it, so internally I might feel a little impatient, like I’m holding onto the thought. But I don’t think I’m usually pushing ahead of people or demanding my turn right away.\n\nWith activities, like waiting in line or waiting for someone else to finish something, I can get annoyed or restless like anyone, but I don’t know that it’s more than normal. It’s not something people have pointed out to me. So I’d say… maybe I feel impatient inside sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed a major problem with waiting my turn.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or blame yourself for things that happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “worthless,” exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do blame myself sometimes, though, especially if something doesn’t get done or I’ve made things harder for someone else. I can go over it in my head and think, “Why didn’t I just handle that better?” or “Why is this so difficult for me when other people seem to manage it?”\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as feeling worthless. I think it’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I feel guilty or ashamed. I can be hard on myself, probably more than I need to be. But if you mean do I sit around believing I have no value as a person, I don’t think I’d describe it that way. It’s more… I disappoint myself, and then I carry that around longer than I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with organization and time management for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not recent. I mean, I’m 70 now, so when I look back it’s a little strange, because for years I thought it was just my personality or that I was messy or not trying hard enough. But it goes way back.\n\nEven when I was a little girl, my teachers would write things like I was bright but careless. That phrase stuck with me, actually — “bright but careless” — because it followed me around in one form or another. I’d forget my books, forget homework, lose track of what I was supposed to bring in, and I remember needing reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. It wasn’t that I didn’t understand the work. A lot of the time I did understand it. I just didn’t have myself organized enough to get it done and turned in the way other children seemed to.\n\nSo, no, this isn’t something that suddenly started in old age or after retirement. It’s been a lifelong pattern, really. The circumstances have changed over the years, but that basic difficulty with keeping things organized and managing what needs to be done has been there since childhood.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I used to think, “Oh, everybody forgets a thing now and then,” but this feels different because it keeps happening with things I really did mean to do.\n\nI’ve missed returning phone calls — someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them back after lunch,” and then somehow it’s two days later and I see the little note sitting there and feel terrible. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. And I just… I couldn’t keep it all straight anymore. I kept worrying I was missing something, and honestly I was missing things. So I ended up giving it up, which was very upsetting for me. It wasn’t just the event—it made me feel like people couldn’t rely on me the way they used to.\n\nAnd with my daughter, that’s been hard too. I think it’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I feel guilty or ashamed. I can be hard on myself, probably more than I need to be. But if you mean do I sit around believing I have no value as a person, I don’t think I’d describe it that way. It’s more… I disappoint myself, and then I carry that around longer than I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with organization and time management for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not recent. I mean, I’m 70 now, so when I look back it’s a little strange, because for years I thought it was just my personality or that I was messy or not trying hard enough. But it goes way back.\n\nEven when I was a little girl, my teachers would write things like I was bright but careless. That phrase stuck with me, actually — “bright but careless” — because it followed me around in one form or another. I’d forget my books, forget homework, lose track of what I was supposed to bring in, and I remember needing reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. It wasn’t that I didn’t understand the work. A lot of the time I did understand it. I just didn’t have myself organized enough to get it done and turned in the way other children seemed to.\n\nSo, no, this isn’t something that suddenly started in old age or after retirement. It’s been a lifelong pattern, really. The circumstances have changed over the years, but that basic difficulty with keeping things organized and managing what needs to be done has been there since childhood.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid your difficulties with organization, focus, and remembering things start when you were a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They definitely started when I was a child. It wasn’t something that appeared later in life.

I remember even as a little girl, teachers would say I was “bright but careless,” which I always found upsetting because I wasn’t trying to be careless. I would forget books, forget homework, and not have the things I needed when I was supposed to have them. And I remember needing reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside, because I’d get off track or just not quite complete what everyone else seemed to finish.

So yes, looking back, the organization and remembering-things part was there very early. It’s not new.

Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. It’s very upsetting, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage simple things, but lately I feel buried by it.\n\nDoctor: How are you feeling emotionally when you're overwhelmed by all these tasks and papers?\n\nPatient: Emotionally? I look at the papers and I know they’re just papers, nothing dangerous, but inside I get this tight, awful feeling like I’ve already failed before I’ve even started.\n\nAnd then I get upset with myself because I think, “Why can’t you just pick one thing and do it?” I’ve managed plenty of things in my life, you know, so it feels embarrassing to be sitting there at my age feeling defeated by a pile on the kitchen table. Sometimes I feel close to tears, not always crying exactly, but like it’s right there under the surface. Mostly I feel stuck and discouraged, and then the longer I sit there, the worse I feel because I can see the day slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very much so. It’s like my attention has become… I don’t know, almost loose. I can sit down with something right in front of me, like a note I’m writing or a plan for the week, and I’ll be trying to concentrate, really trying, and then if the television is on in the next room, even not very loud, my mind just sort of goes over there. I’ll start listening to the voices or the music without meaning to.\n\nAnd at the senior center, if I’m filling something out or trying to keep track of what I was going to do, and two people nearby start talking, that’s it. My brain follows their conversation instead. Not because I’m trying to be nosy, it just pulls me away. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place completely, and I have to figure out what I was writing or planning all over again. It’s frustrating because it feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts if anything else is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you were supposed to do or appointments you had scheduled?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, and it’s become a real problem. I used to think, “Oh, everybody forgets a thing now and then,” but this feels different because it keeps happening with things I really did mean to do.\n\nI’ve missed returning phone calls — someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them back after lunch,” and then somehow it’s two days later and I see the little note sitting there and feel terrible. Bills have gotten left under a stack of mail because I opened them, meant to set them aside somewhere important, and then they just disappeared under everything else on the table.\n\nAnd with my daughter, I’ve forgotten errands I promised her I’d do, which bothers me because I don’t want her thinking I don’t care or that I’m not listening. I really am listening in the moment. It just doesn’t seem to stay where I can get to it later.\n\nThe worst is with my husband’s appointments. More than once I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of them. I’ll have the paper, or the number written down, and I’ll tell myself I need to call, and then the day goes by and I haven’t done it. Then I feel awful, because those things matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t know if I would call it anxiety exactly. I do get worked up, certainly, especially when there’s too much in front of me or I feel like I’m not keeping up. But when you say “anxious,” I think of someone worrying constantly about something specific, and I don’t know that I have it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I get tense and unsettled, and then I start thinking I should be doing better than I am. I can sort of feel it in my body, that tightness, but I don’t always know whether that’s anxiety or just frustration, or feeling overwhelmed. Sometimes it all runs together, honestly. So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I really do, and it’s not even always because the task is physically hard or anything like that. It’s the ones where I know I have to sit and think carefully, and keep track of what each part means. Those are the ones I start avoiding.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know that I would say I’ve been “depressed” in the way people mean it clinically. I do get discouraged, especially when I feel like I’m not managing things the way I should, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being sad or depressed for a period of time.\n\nThere are days when I feel down on myself, like I’ve let people down or I’m just not doing a very good job keeping up with life. But it feels more tied to whatever has happened that day, if that makes sense, rather than a heavy sadness that stays for weeks. At least I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d say I have moments of feeling low or frustrated with myself, but I haven’t really thought of it as depression. Maybe I’m not recognizing it correctly, but nothing comes to mind where I’d say, yes, I was clearly depressed for a long stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family or friends due to your current struggles?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has affected things, more than I like to admit. I try not to make it everyone else’s problem, but it has started spilling over.\n\nThe biggest thing that comes to mind is the senior center. I was supposed to lead this larger event there—well, I had been involved before, and people sort of expected me to help organize it, keep track of the pieces, who was doing what, times, calls, that sort of thing. And I just… I couldn’t keep it all straight anymore. I kept worrying I was missing something, and honestly I was missing things. So I ended up giving it up, which was very upsetting for me. It wasn’t just the event—it made me feel like people couldn’t rely on me the way they used to.\n\nAnd with my daughter, that’s been hard too. She’s kind about it, mostly, but she has started double-checking my bills and my appointments. Not in a cruel way, but I can tell she doesn’t fully trust that I’m going to keep everything organized on my own. She’ll ask, “Did you pay that?” or “Are you sure that appointment is Tuesday?” and sometimes she’ll look it over herself. I know she’s trying to help, but it makes me feel… smaller, I guess. Like I’m being watched.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has caused some strain. Not arguments all the time, exactly, but embarrassment and tension, and this feeling that I’m losing a role I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your struggles with organization and focus have significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just little things, you know, misplacing a paper or needing to write something down, but it’s become more than that. It’s changed what I feel able to take on.\n\nThe senior center event is probably the clearest example. I had been someone people looked to for that sort of thing, helping organize a bigger event, keeping track of who was doing what, the schedule, the calls, all those moving parts. And I just couldn’t keep it straight anymore. I’d sit with the notes and still feel like I was losing pieces of it. Eventually I had to give up leading it, and that was really upsetting. It made me feel like I wasn’t dependable in a way I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’ll ask if I paid something, or she’ll look at the calendar herself because she’s not sure I’ve got it right. She’s not being unkind, but it still feels awful. It makes me feel like I can’t be fully trusted with my own life, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it has affected my daily life, and my well-being too. I feel embarrassed and less confident, and I don’t feel like myself in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you think you should be doing better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean… maybe sometimes, but not in a constant way where I’m walking around feeling guilty all day. It’s more that I get disappointed in myself, or embarrassed, when I realize I didn’t handle something the way I think I should have.\n\nLike I’ll think, “You should be able to do this,” or “Why is this so hard for you now?” And that can feel pretty bad. But guilt, to me, sounds like I’ve done something wrong to someone on purpose, and I don’t usually feel it that way. It’s more of a nagging feeling that I ought to be doing better, that I should have kept track of things or followed through more smoothly.\n\nSo… yes and no. I don’t know if I’d use the word guilty, but I do beat myself up a bit. Maybe more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I don’t always notice it right away. It’s more that I catch myself doing things with my hands or feet when I’m supposed to be sitting still.\n\nLike if I’m in a meeting, or somewhere I’m trying to sit politely and pay attention, I’ll realize I’ve been rubbing my hands together, or tapping my foot, or clicking a pen if I have one. Sometimes I shift around in the chair so much that I can tell people notice—I’ll see someone glance over, and then I feel embarrassed and try to stop. But then a few minutes later I’m doing something else, like moving my fingers or adjusting how I’m sitting again.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be disruptive. It’s almost like my body is busy even when I’m trying to look calm. And at my age, I feel a little silly saying that, but yes, I would say I do get restless or fidgety.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do that very often, no. At least, not in the way I think you mean, like jumping in over people all the time or answering before they’ve even finished. I’m usually more worried about saying the wrong thing, honestly, so I tend to wait.\n\nThat said, sometimes in a conversation I may start to speak because I think the person is finished, and then I realize they weren’t, and I feel bad and stop myself. Or I’ll have a thought come into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I might start to say it a little too quickly. But I wouldn’t say people complain that I interrupt them a lot, and I don’t think of myself as blurting things out.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the bigger problems I notice.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with tasks that require sustained mental effort over a long period?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it’s not like the task is physically hard, you know? It’s paperwork, or reading something carefully, or organizing information, and I’ll look at it and feel tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I can almost feel my mind backing away from it. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just make some tea first,” or “I need to clear off the table,” or “I’ll do it after lunch when I can concentrate better.” And then after lunch there’s another reason, and then it becomes tomorrow. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just avoiding responsibility, but inside it feels like my head is already worn out just thinking about all the little parts I have to keep track of.\n\nEven something like putting together a schedule for a program, where I have to look at times and names and make sure everything lines up, it can feel like too much mental lifting. I know it needs to be done, and I know I’m capable in some basic sense, but sustaining that kind of effort for a long stretch is very difficult for me. I keep drifting away from it or finding small reasons to stop before I really get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I don’t think of myself as someone who has a big problem waiting my turn, at least not in an obvious way.\n\nIn conversations, I usually try to be polite and let people finish. Sometimes I may be sitting there with something I want to say and I’m afraid I’ll forget it, so internally I might feel a little impatient, like I’m holding onto the thought. But I don’t think I’m usually pushing ahead of people or demanding my turn right away.\n\nWith activities, like waiting in line or waiting for someone else to finish something, I can get annoyed or restless like anyone, but I don’t know that it’s more than normal. It’s not something people have pointed out to me. So I’d say… maybe I feel impatient inside sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed a major problem with waiting my turn.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or blame yourself for things that happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “worthless,” exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do blame myself sometimes, though, especially if something doesn’t get done or I’ve made things harder for someone else. I can go over it in my head and think, “Why didn’t I just handle that better?” or “Why is this so difficult for me when other people seem to manage it?”\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as feeling worthless. I think it’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I feel guilty or ashamed. I can be hard on myself, probably more than I need to be. But if you mean do I sit around believing I have no value as a person, I don’t think I’d describe it that way. It’s more… I disappoint myself, and then I carry that around longer than I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with organization and time management for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not recent. I mean, I’m 70 now, so when I look back it’s a little strange, because for years I thought it was just my personality or that I was messy or not trying hard enough. But it goes way back.\n\nEven when I was a little girl, my teachers would write things like I was bright but careless. That phrase stuck with me, actually — “bright but careless” — because it followed me around in one form or another. I’d forget my books, forget homework, lose track of what I was supposed to bring in, and I remember needing reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. It wasn’t that I didn’t understand the work. A lot of the time I did understand it. I just didn’t have myself organized enough to get it done and turned in the way other children seemed to.\n\nSo, no, this isn’t something that suddenly started in old age or after retirement. It’s been a lifelong pattern, really. The circumstances have changed over the years, but that basic difficulty with keeping things organized and managing what needs to be done has been there since childhood.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulties with organization, focus, and remembering things start when you were a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: They definitely started when I was a child. It wasn’t something that appeared later in life.\n\nI remember even as a little girl, teachers would say I was “bright but careless,” which I always found upsetting because I wasn’t trying to be careless. I would forget books, forget homework, and not have the things I needed when I was supposed to have them. And I remember needing reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside, because I’d get off track or just not quite complete what everyone else seemed to finish.\n\nSo yes, looking back, the organization and remembering-things part was there very early. It’s not new.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I was never diagnosed with anything like that when I was young. Back then, I don’t think people were really looking at girls like me that way, or at least no one ever explained it that way to me.

But the problems were there, yes. Even when I was a little girl, teachers would write comments that I was “bright but careless,” and I remember that very clearly because it felt unfair somehow. I wasn’t trying not to care. I would forget books, forget homework, not have what I needed, and then feel embarrassed because other children seemed to manage it more easily.

And I remember needing reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. That happened enough that it stands out in my mind. So I didn’t have a diagnosis as a child or teenager, but the kinds of difficulties you’re asking about were definitely there from early on.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, R41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It’s like everything piles up, and I don’t mean just a little clutter. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and all these lists I start and don’t finish. I’ll sit down thinking, “All right, I’m going to take care of this today,” and then I just stare at everything because I can’t figure out what needs doing first.\n\nAnd then I’ll pick up one paper, put it down, start looking at another one, think maybe I should make a new list, and before I know it the whole day has gone by and nothing is really done. It’s very upsetting, honestly, because I feel like I should be able to manage simple things, but lately I feel buried by it.\n\nDoctor: How are you feeling emotionally when you're overwhelmed by all these tasks and papers?\n\nPatient: Emotionally? I’m not even sure I know how to put it neatly. It’s not just one feeling. I suppose I feel… flustered, and then ashamed that I’m flustered, if that makes sense. I look at the papers and I know they’re just papers, nothing dangerous, but inside I get this tight, awful feeling like I’ve already failed before I’ve even started.\n\nAnd then I get upset with myself because I think, “Why can’t you just pick one thing and do it?” I’ve managed plenty of things in my life, you know, so it feels embarrassing to be sitting there at my age feeling defeated by a pile on the kitchen table. Sometimes I feel close to tears, not always crying exactly, but like it’s right there under the surface. Mostly I feel stuck and discouraged, and then the longer I sit there, the worse I feel because I can see the day slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very much so. It’s like my attention has become… I don’t know, almost loose. I can sit down with something right in front of me, like a note I’m writing or a plan for the week, and I’ll be trying to concentrate, really trying, and then if the television is on in the next room, even not very loud, my mind just sort of goes over there. I’ll start listening to the voices or the music without meaning to.\n\nAnd at the senior center, if I’m filling something out or trying to keep track of what I was going to do, and two people nearby start talking, that’s it. My brain follows their conversation instead. Not because I’m trying to be nosy, it just pulls me away. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place completely, and I have to figure out what I was writing or planning all over again. It’s frustrating because it feels like I can’t keep hold of my own thoughts if anything else is happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you were supposed to do or appointments you had scheduled?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, and it’s become a real problem. I used to think, “Oh, everybody forgets a thing now and then,” but this feels different because it keeps happening with things I really did mean to do.\n\nI’ve missed returning phone calls — someone will leave a message, and I’ll think, “I’ll call them back after lunch,” and then somehow it’s two days later and I see the little note sitting there and feel terrible. Bills have gotten left under a stack of mail because I opened them, meant to set them aside somewhere important, and then they just disappeared under everything else on the table.\n\nAnd with my daughter, I’ve forgotten errands I promised her I’d do, which bothers me because I don’t want her thinking I don’t care or that I’m not listening. I really am listening in the moment. It just doesn’t seem to stay where I can get to it later.\n\nThe worst is with my husband’s appointments. More than once I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of them. I’ll have the paper, or the number written down, and I’ll tell myself I need to call, and then the day goes by and I haven’t done it. Then I feel awful, because those things matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t know if I would call it anxiety exactly. I do get worked up, certainly, especially when there’s too much in front of me or I feel like I’m not keeping up. But when you say “anxious,” I think of someone worrying constantly about something specific, and I don’t know that I have it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like I get tense and unsettled, and then I start thinking I should be doing better than I am. I can sort of feel it in my body, that tightness, but I don’t always know whether that’s anxiety or just frustration, or feeling overwhelmed. Sometimes it all runs together, honestly. So… yes, maybe at times, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m an anxious person all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I really do, and it’s not even always because the task is physically hard or anything like that. It’s the ones where I know I have to sit and think carefully, and keep track of what each part means. Those are the ones I start avoiding.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I’ll put them on the table and tell myself I’m going to do it after breakfast. Then after breakfast I think, well, I should clear the dishes first, or maybe I should make a cup of tea, or I’ll just check something else before I start. And then suddenly half the day is gone and the papers are still sitting there. It’s like my head feels tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nSame with putting together a schedule for a program or anything with dates and times and little details that have to line up. I’ll look at it and just feel this heaviness, like, “Oh, I can’t face this right now.” I keep finding little reasons to delay it, even though I know delaying it usually makes me feel worse. It’s embarrassing, because on the outside it probably looks like I’m just procrastinating, but inside it feels more like I can’t get my mind to climb the hill to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or depressed for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know that I would say I’ve been “depressed” in the way people mean it clinically. I do get discouraged, especially when I feel like I’m not managing things the way I should, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being sad or depressed for a period of time.\n\nThere are days when I feel down on myself, like I’ve let people down or I’m just not doing a very good job keeping up with life. But it feels more tied to whatever has happened that day, if that makes sense, rather than a heavy sadness that stays for weeks. At least I don’t think so.\n\nI guess I’d say I have moments of feeling low or frustrated with myself, but I haven’t really thought of it as depression. Maybe I’m not recognizing it correctly, but nothing comes to mind where I’d say, yes, I was clearly depressed for a long stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family or friends due to your current struggles?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has affected things, more than I like to admit. I try not to make it everyone else’s problem, but it has started spilling over.\n\nThe biggest thing that comes to mind is the senior center. I was supposed to lead this larger event there—well, I had been involved before, and people sort of expected me to help organize it, keep track of the pieces, who was doing what, times, calls, that sort of thing. And I just… I couldn’t keep it all straight anymore. I kept worrying I was missing something, and honestly I was missing things. So I ended up giving it up, which was very upsetting for me. It wasn’t just the event—it made me feel like people couldn’t rely on me the way they used to.\n\nAnd with my daughter, that’s been hard too. She’s kind about it, mostly, but she has started double-checking my bills and my appointments. Not in a cruel way, but I can tell she doesn’t fully trust that I’m going to keep everything organized on my own. She’ll ask, “Did you pay that?” or “Are you sure that appointment is Tuesday?” and sometimes she’ll look it over herself. I know she’s trying to help, but it makes me feel… smaller, I guess. Like I’m being watched.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has caused some strain. Not arguments all the time, exactly, but embarrassment and tension, and this feeling that I’m losing a role I used to have.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your struggles with organization and focus have significantly impacted your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just little things, you know, misplacing a paper or needing to write something down, but it’s become more than that. It’s changed what I feel able to take on.\n\nThe senior center event is probably the clearest example. I had been someone people looked to for that sort of thing, helping organize a bigger event, keeping track of who was doing what, the schedule, the calls, all those moving parts. And I just couldn’t keep it straight anymore. I’d sit with the notes and still feel like I was losing pieces of it. Eventually I had to give up leading it, and that was really upsetting. It made me feel like I wasn’t dependable in a way I used to be.\n\nAnd at home, my daughter has started checking behind me with bills and appointments. She’ll ask if I paid something, or she’ll look at the calendar herself because she’s not sure I’ve got it right. She’s not being unkind, but it still feels awful. It makes me feel like I can’t be fully trusted with my own life, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it has affected my daily life, and my well-being too. I feel embarrassed and less confident, and I don’t feel like myself in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you think you should be doing better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I mean… maybe sometimes, but not in a constant way where I’m walking around feeling guilty all day. It’s more that I get disappointed in myself, or embarrassed, when I realize I didn’t handle something the way I think I should have.\n\nLike I’ll think, “You should be able to do this,” or “Why is this so hard for you now?” And that can feel pretty bad. But guilt, to me, sounds like I’ve done something wrong to someone on purpose, and I don’t usually feel it that way. It’s more of a nagging feeling that I ought to be doing better, that I should have kept track of things or followed through more smoothly.\n\nSo… yes and no. I don’t know if I’d use the word guilty, but I do beat myself up a bit. Maybe more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I don’t always notice it right away. It’s more that I catch myself doing things with my hands or feet when I’m supposed to be sitting still.\n\nLike if I’m in a meeting, or somewhere I’m trying to sit politely and pay attention, I’ll realize I’ve been rubbing my hands together, or tapping my foot, or clicking a pen if I have one. Sometimes I shift around in the chair so much that I can tell people notice—I’ll see someone glance over, and then I feel embarrassed and try to stop. But then a few minutes later I’m doing something else, like moving my fingers or adjusting how I’m sitting again.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be disruptive. It’s almost like my body is busy even when I’m trying to look calm. And at my age, I feel a little silly saying that, but yes, I would say I do get restless or fidgety.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do that very often, no. At least, not in the way I think you mean, like jumping in over people all the time or answering before they’ve even finished. I’m usually more worried about saying the wrong thing, honestly, so I tend to wait.\n\nThat said, sometimes in a conversation I may start to speak because I think the person is finished, and then I realize they weren’t, and I feel bad and stop myself. Or I’ll have a thought come into my head and I’m afraid I’ll lose it, so I might start to say it a little too quickly. But I wouldn’t say people complain that I interrupt them a lot, and I don’t think of myself as blurting things out.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the bigger problems I notice.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with tasks that require sustained mental effort over a long period?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it’s not like the task is physically hard, you know? It’s paperwork, or reading something carefully, or organizing information, and I’ll look at it and feel tired before I’ve even begun.\n\nLike if there’s a form to fill out, or insurance papers to read through, I can almost feel my mind backing away from it. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just make some tea first,” or “I need to clear off the table,” or “I’ll do it after lunch when I can concentrate better.” And then after lunch there’s another reason, and then it becomes tomorrow. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just avoiding responsibility, but inside it feels like my head is already worn out just thinking about all the little parts I have to keep track of.\n\nEven something like putting together a schedule for a program, where I have to look at times and names and make sure everything lines up, it can feel like too much mental lifting. I know it needs to be done, and I know I’m capable in some basic sense, but sustaining that kind of effort for a long stretch is very difficult for me. I keep drifting away from it or finding small reasons to stop before I really get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I don’t think of myself as someone who has a big problem waiting my turn, at least not in an obvious way.\n\nIn conversations, I usually try to be polite and let people finish. Sometimes I may be sitting there with something I want to say and I’m afraid I’ll forget it, so internally I might feel a little impatient, like I’m holding onto the thought. But I don’t think I’m usually pushing ahead of people or demanding my turn right away.\n\nWith activities, like waiting in line or waiting for someone else to finish something, I can get annoyed or restless like anyone, but I don’t know that it’s more than normal. It’s not something people have pointed out to me. So I’d say… maybe I feel impatient inside sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed a major problem with waiting my turn.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or blame yourself for things that happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “worthless,” exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do blame myself sometimes, though, especially if something doesn’t get done or I’ve made things harder for someone else. I can go over it in my head and think, “Why didn’t I just handle that better?” or “Why is this so difficult for me when other people seem to manage it?”\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as feeling worthless. I think it’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I feel guilty or ashamed. I can be hard on myself, probably more than I need to be. But if you mean do I sit around believing I have no value as a person, I don’t think I’d describe it that way. It’s more… I disappoint myself, and then I carry that around longer than I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with organization and time management for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not recent. I mean, I’m 70 now, so when I look back it’s a little strange, because for years I thought it was just my personality or that I was messy or not trying hard enough. But it goes way back.\n\nEven when I was a little girl, my teachers would write things like I was bright but careless. That phrase stuck with me, actually — “bright but careless” — because it followed me around in one form or another. I’d forget my books, forget homework, lose track of what I was supposed to bring in, and I remember needing reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. It wasn’t that I didn’t understand the work. A lot of the time I did understand it. I just didn’t have myself organized enough to get it done and turned in the way other children seemed to.\n\nSo, no, this isn’t something that suddenly started in old age or after retirement. It’s been a lifelong pattern, really. The circumstances have changed over the years, but that basic difficulty with keeping things organized and managing what needs to be done has been there since childhood.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulties with organization, focus, and remembering things start when you were a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: They definitely started when I was a child. It wasn’t something that appeared later in life.\n\nI remember even as a little girl, teachers would say I was “bright but careless,” which I always found upsetting because I wasn’t trying to be careless. I would forget books, forget homework, and not have the things I needed when I was supposed to have them. And I remember needing reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside, because I’d get off track or just not quite complete what everyone else seemed to finish.\n\nSo yes, looking back, the organization and remembering-things part was there very early. It’s not new.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever receive a diagnosis of ADHD or experience significant symptoms of ADHD during your childhood or adolescence?\n\nPatient: No, I was never diagnosed with anything like that when I was young. Back then, I don’t think people were really looking at girls like me that way, or at least no one ever explained it that way to me.\n\nBut the problems were there, yes. Even when I was a little girl, teachers would write comments that I was “bright but careless,” and I remember that very clearly because it felt unfair somehow. I wasn’t trying not to care. I would forget books, forget homework, not have what I needed, and then feel embarrassed because other children seemed to manage it more easily.\n\nAnd I remember needing reminders to finish my seatwork before I could go outside. That happened enough that it stands out in my mind. So I didn’t have a diagnosis as a child or teenager, but the kinds of difficulties you’re asking about were definitely there from early on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
